B routine priorityPublic-health news

Lancaster County coroner says measles caused second infant death as US cases soar past 3,100

News Brief
In Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, a measles-related infant death was confirmed by the coroner, Stephen Diamantoni, MD, on August 18, 2026. This marks the second such death in the county, with the first attributed to a ruptured spleen rather than measles. The CDC has not included these deaths in its 2026 measles fatality count. As of August 26, 2026, the US measles case count reached 3,134, with 115 new cases reported in Pennsylvania. Eight percent of patients were hospitalized, and 94% of cases involved unvaccinated or unknown vaccination status individuals. The Pennsylvania Department of Health warned about potential exposure at the New Stanton Service Plaza on the Pennsylvania Turnpike. The case was initially managed by the Clinic for Special Children before being referred to the coroner’s office. The uncertainty surrounding the infant’s infection timeline remains unresolved, and the broader public health response includes urging vigilance among travelers ahead of the Labor Day weekend.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Main navigation News Antimicrobial Resistance COVID-19 Chronic Wasting Disease Influenza Public Health Alerts All Topics A-Z Coronavirus Vaccines Roadmap Influenza Vaccines Roadmap Roadmaps for Priority Pathogens Vaccine Integrity Project CIDRAP Leadership Forum . . Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search CLOSE Search Support Menu Lancaster County coroner says measles caused second infant death as US cases soar past 3,100 Mary Van Beusekom, MS Measles CDC Share Copied to clipboard Today in Pennsylvania, Lancaster County Coroner Stephen Diamantoni, MD, confirmed that the county’s second measles infant death, which he initially called “measles associated,” was caused by the highly contagious virus. The county is the site of a rapidly growing measles outbreak. Earlier this week, Diamantoni confirmed that measles was detected in a child who had died a few weeks ago. When the Pennsylvania Department of Health confirmed two measles-related deaths last week, it provided little information on the patients, including their ages. Diamantoni has said that the death of the first infant in Lancaster County was not from measles; he attributed it to a ruptured spleen. Measles virus was detected in the boy’s body. He was born to a measles-infected mother on August 14 and died shortly after birth. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) currently does not include either death in its 2026 measles fatality count. At a press briefing today, Diamantoni said the second death involved an Amish girl who was born in July and died on August 18. She had a rare genetic neurologic disorder called Amish lethal microcephaly characterized by abnormally small head size and an underdeveloped brain. She was immediately admitted to palliative care at a hospital. It’s unknown whether the infant was infected with measles before or after birth. Microencephaly may portend short life Microcephaly occurs in about one of every 500 births in the Amish population but is very rare outside of that community, Diamantoni said. “Those children are sick from birth and actually are placed on palliative care from the time of birth,” he said. “They typically only live 5 or 6 months of age and the majority of those children die as a result of a respiratory infection of any kind, because their brain is very small and they don’t have the capacity to fight those infections well.” Many of these children end up dying of respiratory infections of different types, and the death was not unexpected. Stephen Diamantoni, MD Diamantoni said he didn’t know whether this was the second measles-related death reported by Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro and Pennsylvania Secretary of Health Debra Bogen, MD, during a press briefing on August 25. The case wasn’t initially reported to Diamantoni’s office; rather, it was handled by the Clinic for Special Children in Leacock Township. On August 26, US Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. suggested that the deaths were fabricated. “I don’t want to go into details in terms of how we were able to ascertain that information, but I mean, certainly the providers there did not think that it was a case that needed to be referred to the coroner’s office because many of these children end up dying of respiratory infections of different types, and the death was not unexpected," Diamantoni said. Pennsylvania adds 115 new cases Today, the CDC updated the country’s measles case count, which stands at 3,134, including the two Pennsylvania deaths. Eight percent of measles patients have been hospitalized. Of the total, 231 infections are new. That compares with 126 new cases last week. Of all cases, 94% occurred in people who were unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination status. All cases but 16 were locally transmitted. In all of 2025, 2,289 measles case were confirmed in the United States. In Pennsylvania, officials today added 115 new cases—about half of all new US infections—from the past week to its state case tally, raising it to 577 cases. None of the state’s cases involve people fully vaccinated against the virus. In total, 98 people (17%) have been hospitalized. Thirty percent of infections occurred in children. The Pennsylvania Department of Health also warned that travelers at the New Stanton Service Plaza on the westbound Pennsylvania Turnpike in Westmoreland County may have been exposed to measles by a person from another state who stopped at the plaza on August 17 and later tested positive for the virus. On the cusp of the Labor Day weekend, which entails travel for many people, healthcare professionals are urging travelers to be vigilant in monitoring for symptoms of the highly contagious disease, such as rash, fever, and red, watery eyes. Ohio is reporting 15 new cases, including five cases in Wayne County, amid its third measles outbreak of the year, raising its tally for the year to 122. B routine priorityPublic-health news

New measles cases associated with metro Atlanta day care outbreak confirmed, health officials say

News Brief
Health officials in Georgia have confirmed three additional measles cases linked to an outbreak at a daycare in the metro Atlanta area, bringing the total to eight cases. The outbreak involves unvaccinated or under-vaccinated children, and the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported over 2,700 measles cases nationwide in 2026, the highest number in recent history. This surge in cases has raised concerns about the country potentially losing its official measles elimination status. Officials are actively notifying individuals who may have been exposed to the virus and advising them to avoid contact with others, especially those with weakened immune systems or younger than one year old. The situation remains under investigation, with limited details available about the spread and impact of the outbreak.
Original source text (English)
Latest U.S. Iran War Lindsay Clancy Trial World Politics Entertainment HealthWatch MoneyWatch Crime Space Sports Brand Studio The Free Press Local News Atlanta Baltimore Bay Area Boston Chicago Colorado Detroit Los Angeles Miami Minnesota New York Philadelphia Pittsburgh Sacramento Texas Live CBS News 24/7 Baltimore Bay Area Boston Chicago Colorado Detroit Los Angeles Miami Minnesota New York Philadelphia Pittsburgh Sacramento Texas 48 Hours 60 Minutes Shows 48 Hours 60 Minutes CBS Evening News CBS Mornings CBS Morning News CBS Reports CBS Saturday Morning The Daily Report The Dish Face the Nation Sunday Morning The Takeout Things That Matter The Uplift CBS News Investigates CBS News Confirmed Podcasts Newsletters Download Our App CBS News Team Executive Team Brand Studio Paramount+ Join Our Talent Community RSS Feeds Atlanta | News Weather Meet Our Team News Tips Contact Us Download with Damez Download Our App Advertise Memorials Watch CBS News Local News New measles cases associated with metro Atlanta day care outbreak confirmed, health officials say By Dan Raby Dan Raby Senior Digital Producer, CBS News Atlanta Dan Raby, the senior digital producer for CBS News Atlanta, has been covering everything happening around Georgia for more than a decade. Read Full Bio Dan Raby September 4, 2026 / 3:55 PM EDT / CBS Atlanta Add CBS News on Google Georgia health officials have confirmed additional measles cases connected to an outbreak of the virus connected with a metro Atlanta day care. On Friday, the Fulton County Department of Health announced that state officials have confirmed three additional cases associated with the outbreak, which brings the number of cases up to eight. Details about the outbreak remain limited, but Fulton County officials say it "is associated with a daycare setting in the metro Atlanta area" and that the children involved were either unvaccinated or not fully vaccinated. County officials reported the first measles case connected with the outbreak on Aug. 18, then confirmed four additional cases on Aug. 28. Health officials are now working to notify individuals who may have been exposed to the virus with the additional cases. The new update brings the number of confirmed cases of measles in Georgia to 14. This year's number of cases is the second-highest number in the last decade, behind the 18 cases reported in 2019. Data from the Atlanta-based Centers for Disease Control and Prevention shows that more than 2,700 cases of measles have been reported this year, making it the highest number in recent history and putting the country at risk of losing its official measles elimination status. Possible signs of measles exposure and what you should do Measles first infects the respiratory tract, then spreads throughout the body, causing a high fever, runny nose, cough, red, watery eyes, and a rash. The rash generally appears three to five days after the first symptoms, beginning as flat red spots on the face and then spreading downward to the neck, trunk, arms, legs, and feet. When the rash appears, the fever may spike over 104 degrees Fahrenheit, according to the CDC. The virus is highly contagious, spreads through the air, and can stay on surfaces for up to two hours after an infected person has been in a room. If you become sick or believe you have been exposed to measles, contact your healthcare provider immediately but do not go to the doctor's office without first letting them know about your symptoms. You can also call the Georgia Department of Public Health's Acute Disease Epidemiology Section at 404-657-2588 during business hours, Monday through Friday, or 1-866-782-4584 after hours on evenings and weekends. Officials say you should also stay at home and avoid contact with other people, especially anyone younger than 1 year old or those who have weakened immune systems. The Associated Press contributed to this report. 3 new measles cases found at metro Atlanta daycare, Ga. health officials say

News Brief
On September 4, 2026, the Georgia Department of Public Health confirmed three new measles cases at a daycare in metro Atlanta, bringing the total to eight. The cases involve children who were either unvaccinated or not fully vaccinated, indicating a potential risk of spread in under-vaccinated communities. The first case was detected in mid-August, and four additional cases were confirmed last week. Health officials have identified these children as close contacts of the initial confirmed case. The outbreak is currently under investigation, with no specific containment measures or resolution mentioned. This highlights the importance of vaccination in preventing measles transmission, especially in settings where children gather.

报告:地方验尸官宣布麻疹死亡

Report: Local coroner announces measles death
新闻简报据《中心广场》报道,宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县一名6周大的女婴因麻疹去世。这起死亡事件发生在上个月,由兰开斯特县验尸官史蒂芬·迪亚蒙托尼在8月25日的新闻发布会上宣布。这名婴儿出生时患有罕见的遗传疾病——阿米什人致命性小头畸形,并接受姑息治疗,预期寿命不足六个月。卫生部门指出,这些死亡是过去35年来首次与麻疹相关。目前,麻疹 暴发已蔓延至宾夕法尼亚州至少36个县,影响了577名居民,其中98人住院治疗。该婴儿的母亲在分娩前几天因麻疹严重病例住院治疗。《中心广场》尚未独立证实该报告,死亡的确切原因以及麻疹病毒是否影响了器官仍不清楚。该报告还指出,阿米什人和门诺会社区的疫苗接种率一直较低,历史平均约为25%。卫生部门批评了美国卫生部长罗伯特·肯尼迪 Jr. 的言论,认为这些言论可能削弱了公众对疫苗接种的信任,这一观点在费城最近的一次新闻发布会上也得到了呼应。

该事件发生在宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县。受影响的人群包括阿米什人社区,其疫苗接种率历史上一直维持在约25%的水平。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Report: Local coroner announces measles death

News Brief
A 6-week-old baby girl died from measles in Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, according to a report by The Center Square. The death, which occurred last month, was announced by the Lancaster County Coroner, Stephen Diamantoni, during an Aug. 25 press conference. The infant was born with a rare genetic condition called Amish lethal microcephaly and was in palliative care with an expected life expectancy of less than six months. The Department of Health noted that these deaths are the first related to measles in 35 years. An ongoing measles outbreak has spread to at least 36 counties in Pennsylvania, affecting 577 residents and hospitalizing 98. The mother was hospitalized for a severe case of measles just days before giving birth. The Center Square has not independently confirmed the report, and the exact cause of death and whether the measles virus affected the organs remain unclear. The report also highlights concerns about low vaccination rates among Amish and Mennonite communities, which have historically hovered around 25%. The Department of Health criticized U.S. Health Secretary Robert Kennedy Jr. for rhetoric that may have lowered public trust in vaccinations, a sentiment echoed during a recent press conference in Philadelphia.
Original source text (English)
(The Center Square) — A 6-week-old baby girl died from measles last month in Lancaster County, though its unclear if this is the second death identified by the Department of Health during an Aug. 25 press conference that's raised more questions than it answered. First reported by LNP , Lancaster County Coroner Stephen Diamantoni said the infant, born with a rare genetic condition called Amish lethal microcephaly, was in palliative care with an anticipated life expectancy of less than six months. He says he doesn't know if the child was born with measles or contracted it later. The Center Square has not independently confirmed the report. The first identified fatality was a newborn baby, also from the county, who tested positive for measles but died from a ruptured spleen and massive blood loss. Diamantoni said the forensic pathologist did not see any indication that the virus affected the organ. The department said during its Aug. 25 press conference that the deaths are the first related to measles in 35 years. An ongoing outbreak has spread to at least 36 of Pennsylvania's 67 counties, sickening 577 residents and hospitalizing 98. Democratic Gov. Josh Shapiro, while announcing the deaths, blasted U.S. Health Secretary Robert Kennedy Jr. for rhetoric and directives that he says have lowered Americans' trust in vaccinations — a sentiment again reiterated at a Thursday press conference in Philadelphia. The MMR vaccine – which stands for Measles, Mumps, and Rubella – isn't administered until infants are at least one year old . An early dose, between 6 and 11 months, is only recommended when active cases exist. A second dose is given before starting elementary school. "I think it's incumbent on the people who had the press conference to provide accurate information," Lancaster County Commissioner Josh Parsons recently told The Center Square . "And that's really the lesson that we learned out of COVID, which is a lot of people are very distrustful of government, very distrustful of medical officials now and health officials, because of what happened during COVID, so you have to be very careful and provide accurate information." An unconfirmed report from The Atlantic identified the family who lost the infant as members of the Amish community. The mother, who requested not to be identified, said she was hospitalized for a severe case of measles just days before she went in labor. Vaccination rates among Amish and Mennonite communities – of which the country's largest concentration lives in Lancaster County – have been historically low , hovering around 25%, according to federal data. "And I think the issue that some have is that they don't like that they choose not to be vaccinated, and you know that's fine," Parsons said. "They cannot like it, but my position is, as American citizens, they have that right to decide that, and we can provide the information that we do. But ultimately, people have the right to decide that." The United Nations General Assembly on Friday endorsed a new world map that represents Africa more accurately in its larger size, a culmination of a recent campaign led by African countries and advocacy groups. Friday’s resolution, which was sponsored by Togo and backed by members of the African Union, was passed with 164 member states voting in favor. The vote encourages governments and institutions around the world to replace the traditional Mercator map, commonly used since the 16th Century, with the Equal Earth projection that advocates and geographers say shows locations more accurately. Pennsylvania measles tracker | Interactive map

News Brief
The Pennsylvania measles tracker provides an interactive map to monitor the outbreak county-by-county, based on the latest case numbers reported by the Pennsylvania Department of Health. A second death linked to measles is being investigated by the Lancaster County Coroner. Measles is a highly contagious virus that can lead to severe complications, including hospitalization and death, with nearly 20% of cases requiring hospitalization and 1 to 3 per 1,000 cases resulting in fatalities. The outbreak is being tracked to understand its spread and impact. Individuals who may have been exposed or are experiencing symptoms are advised to contact their healthcare provider promptly. The information is sourced from a partial content extract, and the full original article could not be retrieved.
Original source text (English)
Pennsylvania measles tracker | Interactive map Pennsylvania measles tracker | Interactive map HARRISBURG, Pa. — Track Pennsylvania’s measles outbreak county-by-county with this updated interactive heat map, built from the latest case numbers reported by the Pennsylvania Department of Health. See where cases have been confirmed across the state. Video above: Lancaster County Coroner is investigating second death of person with measles. This content is imported from infogram. You may be able to find the same content in another format, or you may be able to find more information, at their web site. Natalya Maisheva Measles rash. About measles Measles is highly contagious and can cause pneumonia, brain swelling, long-term complications, and, in rare cases, death. Nearly 20% of people with measles are hospitalized ; about 1 to 3 per 1,000 cases result in death. The virus spreads through breathing, coughing, or sneezing and can remain infectious in the air or on surfaces for up to two hours . Early symptoms include fever, cough, runny nose, and red, watery eyes . A rash typically appears a few days later, beginning on the head and spreading downward. Symptoms may develop 7–21 days after exposure . If you may have been exposed or develop symptoms, contact your health care provider promptly or call 877-PA-HEALTH (877-724-3258)

Experts alarmed by CDC choice to question deaths associated with measles
新闻简报公共卫生专家对美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 决定对宾夕法尼亚州 麻疹 麻疹 相关的两起死亡事件进行调查的决定表示震惊。这些死亡病例将标志着美国 2026 年首例与 麻疹 相关的死亡病例,但该机构没有像往常一样将这些死亡病例列在 CDC 网站上,而是发布了一份通知,表示他们正在审查这些病例。这种不寻常的做法引发了人们对透明度和潜在政治干预的担忧。疾病预防控制中心前主任德米特·达斯卡拉基斯博士在与卫生部长罗伯特·F·肯尼迪发生冲突后辞职,他称目前的情况很危险。疾病预防控制中心发言人强调,这些信息反映了现有的最佳信息,并且该机构致力于提高透明度。疾病预防控制中心的行动表明这些案件正在调查中,并且回应被认为是初步的。宾夕法尼亚州的 麻疹 暴发 强调了该疾病的持续风险,特别是在 疫苗接种 发病率较低的社区。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Experts alarmed by CDC choice to question deaths associated with measles

News Brief
Public health experts have expressed alarm over the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention's (CDC) decision to question two deaths linked to a measles outbreak in Pennsylvania. These deaths would mark the first measles-related fatalities of 2026 in the U.S., but instead of listing them on the CDC website as usual, the agency posted a notice stating they were reviewing the cases. This unusual approach has raised concerns about transparency and potential political interference. Dr. Demetre Daskalakis, a former CDC director, resigned after clashing with Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, and he described the situation as dangerous. A CDC spokeswoman emphasized that the information reflects the best available and that the agency is committed to transparency. The CDC's actions suggest the cases are under investigation, and the response is considered preliminary. The measles outbreak in Pennsylvania highlights the ongoing risks of the disease, particularly in communities with low vaccination rates.
Original source text (English)
Many public health experts are alarmed by a decision by the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention to question two deaths that Pennsylvania officials say are associated with an ongoing measles outbreak. The deaths would be the first this year linked to a resurgence of measles in the U.S., but instead of listing the deaths on the CDC website as usual, the agency took the highly unusual step of posting a notice that they were reviewing the cases. "That smacks of political interference," said Dr. Demetre Daskalakis, the former director of the CDC's National Center for Immunization and Respiratory Diseases, resigned from the agency last year after clashing with Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. "Something's fishy, and this is dangerous." In an email to NPR, a spokeswoman for Kennedy said the information on the CDC's website "reflects the best available information and our commitment to transparency and telling the American people the truth." An NPR editor adapted this audio report by Rob Stein. NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. The CDC has not listed these deaths on their website as usual, but instead posted a notice about reviewing the cases. This is considered a confirmed event based on the evidence provided.
Scale, impact and risk
The deaths would be the first this year linked to a resurgence of measles in the U.S. The CDC's actions have raised concerns about transparency and potential political interference. The risk is not explicitly stated, but the situation is described as dangerous by some experts.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC is reviewing the cases, and a spokeswoman stated that the information reflects the best available and is transparent. However, the situation remains under investigation, and the response is considered preliminary due to the unusual handling of the deaths.
